People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

I… I don’t know that I’d say completely unable, but it’s harder than it should be. My mind just doesn’t hold onto the numbers the way it used to. I can sit down with something simple, even just writing a note for the youth maker program, and the words and numbers sort of scatter on me.

Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have your memory and thinking problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it has been getting worse, though I don’t want to overstate it. It’s just that my mind doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to anymore.\n\nI came in because of that, really — I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. It’s embarrassing to say, but that’s what it feels like. Is that the kind of worsening you mean?\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking, do you need help with basic daily tasks like dressing, bathing, or eating?\n\nPatient: No… not really with things like dressing or eating or bathing. I can manage those, as far as I know.\n\nIt’s more the other day-to-day responsibilities that have slipped. I’ve let church things, the garden, and the youth maker program fall away more than I’m proud of, and my wife and our pastor have had to step in. And the workshop… I don’t feel quite safe being in there by myself lately, so I’ve needed help around that. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Has your memory trouble made you unable to manage your finances on your own?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say completely unable, but it’s harder than it should be. My mind just doesn’t hold onto the numbers the way it used to. I can sit down with something simple, even just writing a note for the youth maker program, and the words and numbers sort of scatter on me.\n\nSo with finances… I’m not as confident on my own as I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Before your memory problems began, did you lose interest or pleasure in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that actually came first, if I’m being honest.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I just stopped wanting to do the things that usually make me feel like myself. I haven’t touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench, I’ve skipped out on choir planning and chess, and even fishing… which I’ve always liked… just sounds like more effort than it’s worth lately.\n\nThat’s not really like me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling that other people are trying to harm you or plot against you?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I can get uneasy or worry I’ve done something wrong, but I haven’t felt like people are plotting against me or trying to hurt me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t want to overstate it, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. And even when my wife is being kind to me, I still feel like I’m sort of… sitting at the bottom of a well, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that you deserve punishment for something you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I feel bad about things sometimes, and I can be hard on myself, but I don’t really think I deserve to be punished. At least, I don’t think so. Is that an okay answer?\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a medical illness that could affect your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I’ve seen doctors for the usual things at my age, but I don’t think anyone has told me I have an illness that would affect my memory or thinking. If there’s something specific you mean, I can try to remember.\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking, have you needed help managing your usual day-to-day tasks?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’ve let some things slip — church responsibilities, the garden, and the youth maker program. My wife and our pastor have had to step in more than I’d like to admit. And… I don’t really feel safe being by myself in the workshop lately, so I’ve needed help around that. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you know what today’s date is?\n\nPatient: I… I’m sorry, I’m not sure right off. I should know that. Dates have been slipping away from me lately — it’s part of why I came in, honestly. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to. Even writing a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to do without thinking, the words and numbers sort of scatter on me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have your memory and thinking problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it has been getting worse, though I don’t want to overstate it. It’s just that my mind doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to anymore.\n\nI came in because of that, really — I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. It’s embarrassing to say, but that’s what it feels like. Is that the kind of worsening you mean?\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking, do you need help with basic daily tasks like dressing, bathing, or eating?\n\nPatient: No… not really with things like dressing or eating or bathing. I can manage those, as far as I know.\n\nIt’s more the other day-to-day responsibilities that have slipped. I’ve let church things, the garden, and the youth maker program fall away more than I’m proud of, and my wife and our pastor have had to step in. And the workshop… I don’t feel quite safe being in there by myself lately, so I’ve needed help around that. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Has your memory trouble made you unable to manage your finances on your own?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say completely unable, but it’s harder than it should be. My mind just doesn’t hold onto the numbers the way it used to. I can sit down with something simple, even just writing a note for the youth maker program, and the words and numbers sort of scatter on me.\n\nSo with finances… I’m not as confident on my own as I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Before your memory problems began, did you lose interest or pleasure in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that actually came first, if I’m being honest.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I just stopped wanting to do the things that usually make me feel like myself. I haven’t touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench, I’ve skipped out on choir planning and chess, and even fishing… which I’ve always liked… just sounds like more effort than it’s worth lately.\n\nThat’s not really like me. Is that the sort of loss of interest you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking, have you ever gotten lost in a place that should have been familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve gotten lost, exactly… not in the sense of not knowing where I am.\n\nIt’s more that my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to do something simple, like write a note for the youth maker program, something I used to do without thinking, and the words or numbers just… scatter on me. I know that’s not quite the same as getting lost in a place, but that’s the kind of thinking trouble I’ve noticed. Is that okay to say?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

My mind just doesn’t hold onto the numbers the way it used to. I can sit down with something simple, even just writing a note for the youth maker program, and the words and numbers sort of scatter on me.\n\nSo with finances… I’m not as confident on my own as I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Before your memory problems began, did you lose interest or pleasure in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that actually came first, if I’m being honest.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I just stopped wanting to do the things that usually make me feel like myself. I haven’t touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench, I’ve skipped out on choir planning and chess, and even fishing… which I’ve always liked… just sounds like more effort than it’s worth lately.\n\nThat’s not really like me. Is that the sort of loss of interest you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking, have you ever gotten lost in a place that should have been familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve gotten lost, exactly… not in the sense of not knowing where I am.\n\nIt’s more that my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to do something simple, like write a note for the youth maker program, something I used to do without thinking, and the words or numbers just… scatter on me. I know that’s not quite the same as getting lost in a place, but that’s the kind of thinking trouble I’ve noticed. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt convinced that something terrible is true about you or your situation, even when other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking.\n\nI do get hard on myself sometimes, and things can feel pretty bleak, but I don’t know that I’ve been convinced of some terrible thing being true when people are telling me it isn’t. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly, yes… I mean, I’m still at home, and I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do anything for myself.

But… some things have slipped. I’ve let my church responsibilities go, and the garden’s kind of fallen away. The youth maker program too… my wife and our pastor have had to step in more than I’d like to admit.

And with the workshop, I don’t really feel safe being in there by myself anymore, so someone else has had to take over or be involved. I’m sorry, I’m not sure if that counts as not living independently, but that’s what’s been happening.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF03, F32.3, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… thank you for seeing me. I’m probably not explaining it very well, but I came in because my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could’ve done without thinking before—and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. It’s… embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you could not remember important parts of your own life, like who you are or major personal events?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I forget small things, and that bothers me, but not things like who I am or the big events in my life. At least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling that other people are trying to harm you or plot against you?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I can get uneasy or worry I’ve done something wrong, but I haven’t felt like people are plotting against me or trying to hurt me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I don’t want to overstate it, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. And even when my wife is being kind to me, I still feel like I’m sort of… sitting at the bottom of a well, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that you deserve punishment for something you’ve done?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I feel bad about things sometimes, and I can be hard on myself, but I don’t really think I deserve to be punished. At least, I don’t think so. Is that an okay answer?\n\nDoctor: Has a doctor told you that you have a medical illness that could affect your memory or thinking?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I’ve seen doctors for the usual things at my age, but I don’t think anyone has told me I have an illness that would affect my memory or thinking. If there’s something specific you mean, I can try to remember.\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking, have you needed help managing your usual day-to-day tasks?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’ve let some things slip — church responsibilities, the garden, and the youth maker program. My wife and our pastor have had to step in more than I’d like to admit. And… I don’t really feel safe being by myself in the workshop lately, so I’ve needed help around that. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you know what today’s date is?\n\nPatient: I… I’m sorry, I’m not sure right off. I should know that. Dates have been slipping away from me lately — it’s part of why I came in, honestly. My mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to. Even writing a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to do without thinking, the words and numbers sort of scatter on me. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have your memory and thinking problems been steadily getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it has been getting worse, though I don’t want to overstate it. It’s just that my mind doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to anymore.\n\nI came in because of that, really — I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. It’s embarrassing to say, but that’s what it feels like. Is that the kind of worsening you mean?\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking, do you need help with basic daily tasks like dressing, bathing, or eating?\n\nPatient: No… not really with things like dressing or eating or bathing. I can manage those, as far as I know.\n\nIt’s more the other day-to-day responsibilities that have slipped. I’ve let church things, the garden, and the youth maker program fall away more than I’m proud of, and my wife and our pastor have had to step in. And the workshop… I don’t feel quite safe being in there by myself lately, so I’ve needed help around that. Is that the distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Has your memory trouble made you unable to manage your finances on your own?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know that I’d say completely unable, but it’s harder than it should be. My mind just doesn’t hold onto the numbers the way it used to. I can sit down with something simple, even just writing a note for the youth maker program, and the words and numbers sort of scatter on me.\n\nSo with finances… I’m not as confident on my own as I used to be. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Before your memory problems began, did you lose interest or pleasure in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that actually came first, if I’m being honest.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I just stopped wanting to do the things that usually make me feel like myself. I haven’t touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench, I’ve skipped out on choir planning and chess, and even fishing… which I’ve always liked… just sounds like more effort than it’s worth lately.\n\nThat’s not really like me. Is that the sort of loss of interest you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking, have you ever gotten lost in a place that should have been familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve gotten lost, exactly… not in the sense of not knowing where I am.\n\nIt’s more that my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to do something simple, like write a note for the youth maker program, something I used to do without thinking, and the words or numbers just… scatter on me. I know that’s not quite the same as getting lost in a place, but that’s the kind of thinking trouble I’ve noticed. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt convinced that something terrible is true about you or your situation, even when other people tell you it isn’t?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking.\n\nI do get hard on myself sometimes, and things can feel pretty bleak, but I don’t know that I’ve been convinced of some terrible thing being true when people are telling me it isn’t. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoes that answer it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Do you know where you are right now?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m at your office, here for this appointment.\n\nAt least, that’s my understanding. I don’t think I’m confused about where I am right now. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Because of your memory or thinking problems, are you still able to live independently without someone regularly taking over your day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, I’m still at home, and I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t do anything for myself.\n\nBut… some things have slipped. I’ve let my church responsibilities go, and the garden’s kind of fallen away. The youth maker program too… my wife and our pastor have had to step in more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAnd with the workshop, I don’t really feel safe being in there by myself anymore, so someone else has had to take over or be involved. I mean, I’ve seen doctors for the usual things at my age, but I don’t think anyone has told me I have an illness that would affect my memory or thinking. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you know what today’s date is?\n\nPatient: I… I’m sorry, I’m not sure right off. I should know that. The trouble with thinking and remembering really seemed to come along after I’d already started feeling so low.\n\nI came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore.
